<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

Uniform property $\Gamma$ asks for central projections that divide every trace while preserving its values on prescribed coefficients. This form of central divisibility is central to the regularity theory of nuclear $C^*$-algebras. It was introduced by Castillejos, Evington, Tikuisis, White and Winter (Castillejos et al. 2021, Definition 2.1) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[CETWW](#ref-CETWW)], and developed systematically by Castillejos, Evington, Tikuisis and White (Castillejos et al. 2022) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[CETW](#ref-CETW)]. For simple separable unital nuclear algebras with traces, strict comparison together with uniform property $\Gamma$ is equivalent to absorption of the Jiang–Su algebra (Castillejos et al. 2022, Theorem 5.6) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[CETW](#ref-CETW)]. We give three independent constructions of this central divisibility. The first derives uniform property $\Gamma$ from real rank zero of a completion ultrapower, answering a question of Schafhauser, Tikuisis and White. The second derives Jiang–Su absorption from strict comparison in the unital setting, completing the unital Toms–Winter equivalence. The third treats stably projectionless algebras with bounded densely finite traces and a compact normalized trace base. The common task is to make projections central while preserving every coefficient trace; the hypotheses and the averaging arguments differ in the three routes.

The underlying finite-factor notion goes back to Murray and von Neumann (Murray and Neumann 1943) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[MurrayVonNeumann1943](#ref-MurrayVonNeumann1943)]. Dixmier developed its central projection formulation (Dixmier 1969) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[Dixmier1969](#ref-Dixmier1969)], while McDuff studied the stronger central matrix structure associated with absorption of the hyperfinite factor (McDuff 1970) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[McDuff1970](#ref-McDuff1970)]. Connes’ classification of injective factors (Connes 1976) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[Connes](#ref-Connes)] supplies the pointwise von Neumann regularity used in our nonunital construction. The uniform $C^*$-algebraic question asks for a single family of projections that works simultaneously across the trace space and retains every coefficient identity. Pointwise tracial approximation must therefore be combined with a uniform construction.

The first construction starts with real rank zero in a uniform tracial ultrapower. A unital $C^*$-algebra has *real rank zero* when its selfadjoint elements can be approximated in norm by selfadjoint elements with finite spectrum (Brown and Pedersen 1991) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[BP91](#ref-BP91)]. We first describe precisely the resulting trace identities. For a unital algebra $A$ with nonempty tracial-state space $T(A)$, put $$\|a\|_{2,T(A)}=\sup_{\tau\in T(A)}\tau(a^*a)^{1/2}.$$ The uniform tracial completion belongs to the framework initiated by Ozawa (Ozawa 2013) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[Ozawa13](#ref-Ozawa13)] and developed in (Carrión et al. 2023) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[CCEGSTW](#ref-CCEGSTW)]. The algebra $B=\overline A^{T(A)}$ consists of bounded uniformly $2$-Cauchy sequences modulo sequences tending to zero in this seminorm. Each $\tau\in T(A)$ extends to a designated trace $\bar\tau$ on $B$. For a fixed free ultrafilter $\omega$ on $\mathbb N$, the uniform tracial ultrapower $B^\omega$ is the quotient of bounded sequences in $B$ by those whose uniform $2$-norm tends to zero along $\omega$. The family $\Lambda_\omega$ consists of exactly the traces $$\lambda([(b_j)])=\lim_{j\to\omega}\bar\tau_j(b_j),
 \qquad \tau_j\in T(A).$$ These constructions and their quotient norms are specified in Section <a href="#r:sec:preliminaries" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="r:sec:preliminaries">2</a>.

<a id="stmt-r-thm-main"></a>
<div id="r:thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1** (Real rank zero and uniform property $\Gamma$). *Let $A$ be a unital, separable, simple, infinite-dimensional, nuclear, stably finite $C^*$-algebra with $T(A)\ne\varnothing$, and let $B=\overline A^{T(A)}$. Fix any free ultrafilter $\omega$ on $\mathbb N$. If $B^\omega$ has real rank zero in its quotient $C^*$-norm, then there is a projection $p\in B^\omega\cap B'$ such that $$\lambda(px)=\tfrac12\lambda(x)
 \qquad(x\in B,\ \lambda\in\Lambda_\omega).$$ In particular, $A$ has uniform property $\Gamma$.*

</div>

This gives a positive resolution of Problem XXI of Schafhauser, Tikuisis and White (Schafhauser, Tikuisis, and White 2026, sec. 6) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[STW](#ref-STW)]. Their Definition 16 uses precisely this completion and halving formulation. In the usual formulation, uniform property $\Gamma$ requires, for every $m\ge1$, orthogonal projections $p_1,\ldots,p_m\in A^\omega\cap A'$ summing to one such that $$\lambda(ap_i)=\frac1m\lambda(a)
 \quad(a\in A,\ \lambda\in T_\omega(A)).$$ Here $A^\omega$ is the uniform tracial ultrapower formed directly from $A$, and $T_\omega(A)$ is its family of varying limit traces. The completion formulation and the all-$m$ formulation agree by (Carrión et al. 2023, Propositions 5.7, 5.20 and 5.23) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[CCEGSTW](#ref-CCEGSTW)]; we record the interfaces beside the definitions in Section <a href="#r:sec:preliminaries" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="r:sec:preliminaries">2</a>. The coefficient $a$ is essential: equality of scalar traces of the $p_i$ alone does not give uniform property $\Gamma$.

The additional centrality in Theorem <a href="#r:thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="r:thm:main">1</a> is the main issue. Vaccaro derives tracial almost divisibility from real rank zero of the tracial ultrapower: hereditary subalgebras admit tracially large matrix order-zero maps, without a centrality requirement (Vaccaro 2026, Proposition 2.2) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[Vac26](#ref-Vac26)]. Combined with tracially locally finite nuclear dimension, this yields uniform McDuffness and hence uniform property $\Gamma$ (Vaccaro 2026, Theorem 2.3) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[Vac26](#ref-Vac26)]. Kessinger and Toms obtain uniform property $\Gamma$ from subquadratic growth in recursive-subhomogeneous inductive limits and from a tracial local approximation condition (Kessinger and Toms 2026, Theorems 1.2 and 7.11) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[KT26](#ref-KT26)]. Their results exclude nonzero finite-dimensional representations; the local theorem also assumes nonempty traces. Their argument supplies both ultrapower real rank zero and tracially locally finite nuclear dimension, then uses this divisibility route. We instead construct central coefficient splittings using nuclear averaging under the hypotheses of Theorem <a href="#r:thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="r:thm:main">1</a>. Nuclearity alone does not ensure uniform property $\Gamma$: Toms constructs a simple separable unital AH algebra without it (Toms 2026, Theorem 1) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[Toms26](#ref-Toms26)].

There are also implications in the other direction. For simple separable unital infinite-dimensional nuclear algebras with traces, Fu proves that uniform property $\Gamma$ implies the real-rank and divisibility conditions of (Fu 2026, Theorems 1.1 and 10.6) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[Fu26](#ref-Fu26)]. Uniform property $\Gamma$ also passes to the tracial completion and supplies complemented partitions of unity (Carrión et al. 2023, Proposition 5.20 and Theorem 1.4) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[CCEGSTW](#ref-CCEGSTW)]. For factorial tracially complete algebras, complemented partitions of unity imply real rank zero of their tracial ultrapowers (Carrión et al. 2023, Proposition 7.2) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[CCEGSTW](#ref-CCEGSTW)]; Evington and Tikuisis strengthen this to real rank zero of the tracially complete algebra itself (Evington and Tikuisis 2026, Theorem A) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[EvingtonTikuisis26](#ref-EvingtonTikuisis26)]. These results assume the uniform partition structure from which projections can be assembled. Theorem <a href="#r:thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="r:thm:main">1</a> constructs central splittings from the ultrapower real-rank-zero assumption.

The same hypotheses also identify all traces on the completion.

<a id="stmt-r-cor-completion-traces"></a>
<div id="r:cor:completion-traces" class="corollary">

**Corollary 2** (Traces on the completion). *Under the hypotheses of Theorem <a href="#r:thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="r:thm:main">1</a>, including real rank zero of $B^\omega$ in its quotient $C^*$-norm, every tracial state of $B$ is the designated extension of a unique trace in $T(A)$. Thus restriction is an affine homeomorphism $T(B)\to T(A)$, with inverse $\tau\mapsto\bar\tau$. Moreover, $$|\sigma(b)|\le\|b\|_{2,T(A)}
 \qquad(b\in B,\ \sigma\in T(B)),$$ so every tracial state of $B$ is continuous for the designated uniform $2$-norm.*

</div>

The proof appears at the end of Section <a href="#r:sec:completion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="r:sec:completion">6</a>.

The construction is useful outside an ultrapower. A projection is called *full* when the closed two-sided ideal it generates is the entire ambient algebra.

<a id="stmt-r-thm-finite"></a>
<div id="r:thm:finite" class="theorem">

**Theorem 3** (Finite-set central splitting). *Let $A\subseteq D$ be a unital inclusion of $C^*$-algebras, with $A$ nuclear, $D$ of real rank zero, and $T(D)\ne\varnothing$. Suppose that for every $\delta>0$ there is a full projection $g\in D$ with $\sup_{\tau\in T(D)}\tau(g)<\delta$. For every finite $K\subseteq\mathcal U(A)$ and $\varepsilon>0$ there is a projection $P\in D$ such that, for every $x\in K$, $$\sup_{\tau\in T(D)}\|[P,x]\|_{2,\tau}<\varepsilon,
 \qquad
 \sup_{\tau\in T(D)}|\tau(Px)-\tfrac12\tau(x)|<\varepsilon.$$*

</div>

The ambient algebra in this result need not be nuclear or simple. Its tracial states need not be faithful. In the application $D=B^\omega$, simplicity and infinite dimensionality of $A$ provide the full projections of small trace uniformly over *all* abstract tracial states of $D$. The last diagonal argument then uses the specified family $\Lambda_\omega$ to obtain a single projection commuting with all of $B$.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Strict comparison and Jiang–Su absorption

The next two results obtain uniform property $\Gamma$ directly from strict comparison. Each supplies its own projections and is independent of the real-rank-zero hypothesis in Theorem <a href="#r:thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="r:thm:main">1</a>. For positive $a,b\in A\otimes\mathcal K$, Cuntz subequivalence $a\precsim b$ means that $x_n^*bx_n\to a$ in norm for a sequence $x_n\in A\otimes\mathcal K$. Here $\mathcal K$ denotes the compact operators, tensor products are spatial, and each trace is extended using the unnormalized matrix trace. Set $$d_\tau(a)=\lim_{n\to\infty}(\tau\otimes\operatorname{Tr})(a^{1/n})\in[0,\infty].$$ For simple unital algebras we use the comparison convention $$b\ne0,\quad d_\tau(a)<d_\tau(b)\ (\tau\in T(A))
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad a\precsim b.$$ If $T(A)$ is empty, this convention requires comparison with every nonzero positive target; it is more than the absence of traces.

The Jiang–Su algebra $\mathcal Z$ was introduced by Jiang and Su (Jiang and Su 1999) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[JS](#ref-JS)]; $\mathcal Z$-stability means $A\cong A\otimes\mathcal Z$. For simple, separable, unital, infinite-dimensional nuclear algebras, the Toms–Winter conjecture asks for the equivalence (Winter and Zacharias 2010, Conjecture 9.3) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[WZNuc](#ref-WZNuc)] $$\text{finite nuclear dimension}
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \mathcal Z\text{-stability}
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \text{strict comparison}.$$ Finite nuclear dimension requires completely positive approximations through finite-dimensional algebras with a fixed finite number of outgoing pieces that preserve orthogonality of positive elements. The implications from finite nuclear dimension to $\mathcal Z$-stability, from $\mathcal Z$-stability to strict comparison, and from $\mathcal Z$-stability to finite nuclear dimension are known (Winter 2012; Rørdam 2004; Castillejos et al. 2021) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[WinterNuc](#ref-WinterNuc), [Rordam](#ref-Rordam), [CETWW](#ref-CETWW)]. Theorem <a href="#u:thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="u:thm:main">4</a> proves the remaining comparison-to-absorption implication, giving a positive resolution in the unital setting. The empty-trace case is included with the comparison convention stated above; its purely infinite absorption argument is given in Section <a href="#u:sec:gamma" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="u:sec:gamma">11</a>.

<a id="stmt-u-thm-main"></a>
<div id="u:thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 4** (The direct unital comparison route). *Let $A$ be a simple, separable, unital, infinite-dimensional nuclear $C^*$-algebra with strict comparison in the preceding sense. Then $A\cong A\otimes\mathcal Z$. When $T(A)\ne\varnothing$, the proof constructs uniform property $\Gamma$ directly.*

</div>

In the tracial case, Matui and Sato established the absorption implication for finitely many extremal traces using comparison and property (SI) (Matui and Sato 2012, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[MS](#ref-MS)]. The compact finite-dimensional boundary case was obtained independently by Kirchberg and Rørdam (Kirchberg and Rørdam 2014, Corollary 7.9) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[KR14](#ref-KR14)], Sato (Sato 2012, Corollary 1.2) <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[Sato12](#ref-Sato12)], and Toms, White and Winter (Toms, White, and Winter 2015, Corollary 4.7) <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[TWW15](#ref-TWW15)]. Later results reach other trace spaces: Lin proves the implication under his condition (C) together with stable rank one, and removes the stable-rank assumption in its countable-boundary case (Lin 2024, Theorem 5.6 and Corollary 5.7) <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[Lin24](#ref-Lin24)]. Condition (C) decomposes the extreme boundary into countably many finite-dimensional compact pieces with additional compatibility requirements on their convex faces (Lin 2024, Definition 2.13) <a id="pm-cite-29"></a>[[Lin24](#ref-Lin24)]. Evington and Schafhauser subsequently obtained uniform property $\Gamma$ from fibrewise property $\Gamma$ under the compact finite-dimensional boundary hypothesis without assuming nuclearity (Evington and Schafhauser 2026, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-30"></a>[[EvingtonSchafhauser26](#ref-EvingtonSchafhauser26)]. The uniform divisibility criterion of (Castillejos et al. 2022, Theorem 5.6) <a id="pm-cite-31"></a>[[CETW](#ref-CETW)] explains why constructing central projections is the decisive task for the comparison-to-absorption implication.

For the nonunital result, write $T_{\mathrm{lsc}}(A)$ for the cone of densely finite lower-semicontinuous traces and $T_1(A)$ for its bounded traces of norm one. The next theorem has a distinct trace hypothesis.

<a id="stmt-n-thm-main"></a>
<div id="n:thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 5** (The direct stably projectionless route). *Let $A$ be a nonzero simple, separable, nuclear $C^*$-algebra such that $A\otimes\mathcal K$ has no nonzero projections. Suppose that every trace in $T_{\mathrm{lsc}}(A)$ is bounded on the positive unit ball and that $T_1(A)$ is nonempty and weak-$*$ compact. Assume that, for $a,b\in(A\otimes\mathcal K)_+$ with $b\ne0$, $$d_\tau(a)<1\quad
 \text{for every }\tau\in T_{\mathrm{lsc}}(A)
 \text{ with }d_\tau(b)=1
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad a\precsim b.$$ Then $A$ has uniform property $\Gamma$ and $A\cong A\otimes\mathcal Z$.*

</div>

Boundedness of the entire densely finite trace cone and compactness of its normalized base are separate assumptions. The comparison tests are exactly those trace rays with positive finite target rank; ranks in the stabilization may be infinite. The construction leads through the nonunital uniform McDuffness and absorption criteria of Castillejos, Evington, Tikuisis and White (Castillejos et al. 2022) <a id="pm-cite-32"></a>[[CETW](#ref-CETW)] and Castillejos, Li and Szabó (Castillejos, Li, and Szabó 2023) <a id="pm-cite-33"></a>[[CLS](#ref-CLS)]. Jacelon’s work on nonunital algebras with finite-dimensional trace boundary and continuous rank functions is an earlier part of this development (Jacelon 2013) <a id="pm-cite-34"></a>[[Jacelon](#ref-Jacelon)].

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### The three constructions and their interfaces

The common problem is to turn projections that split traces into projections that are central and split *coefficient* traces. Figure <a href="#fig:routes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:routes">1</a> shows the separate routes. Each is proved in full, and the first uses no comparison assumption.

<figure id="fig:routes">

<figcaption>Three routes to central coefficient-tested trace splitting. The upper route uses real rank zero of the completion ultrapower. The lower routes use comparison and supply their own projections in $A^{ω}$. Absorption is a consequence of the two comparison routes through the published criteria verified in the text.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-routes"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:routes`)

TikZ/PGF source, copied verbatim from the source:

 box/.style={draw,rounded corners=2pt,align=center,text width=42mm,
 minimum height=13mm,inner sep=4pt}, >={Stealth[length=2mm]}]
\node[box] (r1) {Real rank zero\\and nuclear averaging};
\node[box,right=of r1] (r2) {Weighted blocks\\and orthogonal packing};
\node[box,right=of r2] (r3) {Halving in $B^\omega$\\against all $x\in B$};
\node[box,below=of r1] (u1) {Unital comparison\\and rank rounding};
\node[box,right=of u1] (u2) {Centered symmetries\\and a Gaussian limit};
\node[box,right=of u2] (u3) {Uniform $\Gamma$\\and absorption};
\node[box,below=of u1] (n1) {Nonunital comparison\\with bounded traces\\and a compact base};

Visual witness description of PDF page 6 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
A flowchart consisting of nine rectangular text boxes arranged in three horizontal rows of three boxes each, connected by horizontal rightward arrows:
- Row 1 (top): Box labeled "Real rank zero and nuclear averaging" with a rightward arrow to box labeled "Weighted blocks and orthogonal packing", which has a rightward arrow to box labeled "Halving in $B^\omega$ against all $x \in B$".
- Row 2 (middle): Box labeled "Unital comparison and rank rounding" with a rightward arrow to box labeled "Centered symmetries and a Gaussian limit", which has a rightward arrow to box labeled "Uniform $\Gamma$ and absorption".
- Row 3 (bottom): Box labeled "Nonunital comparison with bounded traces and a compact base" with a rightward arrow to box labeled "Cost selection and bounded truncation", which has a rightward arrow to box labeled "Uniform $\Gamma$ and nonunital absorption".
```


For the real-rank-zero route, Haagerup’s convex virtual diagonal (Haagerup 1983, Theorem 3.1) <a id="pm-cite-35"></a>[[Haa83](#ref-Haa83)] provides nuclear averaging maps chosen before any input projection. Finite scalar compressions turn their outputs into weighted matrix blocks. A small proportion of a base projection can be sampled inside each block without making that subprojection central: scalar matrix compressions retain the coefficient traces and squared commutators. Orthogonal insertion then packs two labelled families with matching target traces. The estimates use total weighted mass and energy, so they remain independent of the number and sizes of the blocks. A bounded diagonal argument yields the desired projection for the original ultrafilter, and uniform $2$-density extends its identities from $A$ to its completion.

For the unital comparison route, a full positive element of small rank receives an exact trace-preserving compression of a given positive element. Integer spectral cuts approximate that trace by a finite-matrix rank, as proved in Section <a href="#u:sec:ranks" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="u:sec:ranks">8</a>; this elementary step needs neither nuclearity nor comparison. Comparison then produces projections with exact spectral support in $A^\omega$. Scalar compressions and matrix-unit transport construct symmetries centered against any prescribed separable coefficient algebra. The convex order-zero approximations of Hirshberg, Kirchberg and White (Hirshberg, Kirchberg, and White 2012, Theorem 1.4) <a id="pm-cite-36"></a>[[HKW](#ref-HKW)] give a square-root-weighted sum of these symmetries with fourth moment at most three. Its norm may grow with the number of summands. Clipping at a fixed radius supplies bounded relative moment estimates; successively adjoining commuting variables gives normalized sums with a common Gaussian limit in coefficient traces. Continuous approximations to equal-measure Gaussian intervals then yield the required central projections.

For the stably projectionless route, spectral allocation supplies small projections with a one-sided cost bound. A single cost records both overlap with earlier projections and error in a finite-dimensional model. A block-matrix functional-calculus estimate passes aggregate squared commutators to a truncated sum without a factor for the number of models. The resulting small splittings have trace and projection errors of order $\lambda^{3/2}$. Reindexing and multiplication amplify them to exact splittings. The final passage distinguishes the uniform tracial ultrapower $A^\omega$, the norm ultrapower $A_\omega$, and the annihilator quotient $F_\omega(A)$, and checks generalized limit traces explicitly.

Sections <a href="#r:sec:preliminaries" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="r:sec:preliminaries">2</a>–<a href="#r:sec:completion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="r:sec:completion">6</a> prove the real-rank-zero route, including the finite-set theorem. Sections <a href="#u:sec:preliminaries" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="u:sec:preliminaries">7</a>–<a href="#u:sec:gamma" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="u:sec:gamma">11</a> give the direct unital route and handle its empty-trace case. Sections <a href="#n:sec:framework" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="n:sec:framework">12</a>– <a href="#n:sec:absorption" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="n:sec:absorption">16</a> give the bounded-trace nonunital route. The elementary small-support lemma in Section <a href="#r:sec:projections" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="r:sec:projections">3</a> serves both unital routes; Section <a href="#u:sec:ranks" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="u:sec:ranks">8</a> proves the trace-transfer and integer-rounding argument at its point of use.

<a id="r:sec:preliminaries"></a>
### Notation and the tracial completion

For a unital $C^*$-algebra $D$, write $\operatorname T(D)$ for its normalized tracial states and $\mathcal U(D)$ for its unitary group. For $\tau\in\operatorname T(D)$, put $$\left\|d\right\|_{1,\tau}=\tau(|d|),\qquad
 \left\|d\right\|_{2,\tau}=\tau(d^*d)^{1/2}.$$ These may be seminorms. We use the finite von Neumann algebra obtained from the tracial GNS representation when taking support projections, polar decompositions, or signs in a trace calculation. In particular, tracial Cauchy–Schwarz and trace-norm duality give <a id="r:eq:trace-inequalities"></a>$$\label{r:eq:trace-inequalities}
 \begin{aligned}
 |\tau(ab)|&\leq\left\|a\right\|_{2,\tau}\left\|b\right\|_{2,\tau},\qquad
 |\tau(ab)|\leq\norm a\left\|b\right\|_{1,\tau},\\
 \norm{adb}_{s,\tau}&\leq\norm a\norm b\norm d_{s,\tau}
 \qquad(s=1,2).
 \end{aligned}$$ In the last expression, $\norm d_{s,\tau}$ denotes the corresponding tracial seminorm. All uses of these inequalities also apply to the unnormalized matrix trace $\operatorname{Tr}\otimes\tau$. We always reserve $\operatorname{Tr}$ for the unnormalized scalar matrix trace. A scalar matrix over a corner $qDq$ is written $G\otimes q$.

A projection is *full* if the closed two-sided ideal it generates is the whole algebra. For projections $e,f\in D$, the notation $e\precsim f$ means that there is a partial isometry $v\in D$ with $v^*v=e$ and $vv^*\leq f$. Thus all projection comparisons used below are implemented inside $D$. Real rank zero means that finite-spectrum self-adjoint elements are norm dense in $D_{\mathrm{sa}}$.

Every contraction in a unital $C^*$-algebra is a linear combination of four unitaries whose coefficient absolute values sum to at most two. Indeed, write $a=h+ik$, where $h,k$ are self-adjoint contractions, and use $$h=\frac{u_h+u_h^*}{2},\qquad
 u_h=h+i(1-h^2)^{1/2},$$ and the same formula for $k$. Consequently, statements about a finite set of elements can be reduced to statements about a finite set of unitaries, with controlled constants.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
#### The specified completion and limit traces

Let $A$ be as in Theorem <a href="#r:thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="r:thm:main">1</a>. For $a\in A$, write $$\left\|a\right\|_{2,\operatorname T(A)}
 =\sup_{\tau\in\operatorname T(A)}\tau(a^*a)^{1/2}.$$ Let $C$ be the $C^*$-algebra of operator-norm-bounded sequences in $A$ which are Cauchy for this norm, and let $J$ consist of those sequences whose uniform tracial 2-norm tends to zero. The uniform tracial completion is $B=C/J$, equipped with its quotient $C^*$-norm. The designated traces on $B$ are precisely $$\bar\tau([(a_j)])=\lim_j\tau(a_j),\qquad \tau\in\operatorname T(A).$$ These extensions define $\left\|\cdot\right\|_{2,\operatorname T(A)}$ on $B$. No additional abstract traces of $B$ enter this notation.

For a fixed free ultrafilter $\omega$ on $\mathbb N$, define <a id="r:eq:ultrapower"></a>$$\label{r:eq:ultrapower}
 B^\omega=\ell^\infty(\mathbb N,B)/I_\omega,\qquad
 I_\omega=\{(b_j):\lim_{j\to\omega}\left\|b_j\right\|_{2,\operatorname T(A)}=0\}.$$ Here and below the sequences in $I_\omega$ are operator-norm bounded. For each sequence $(\tau_j)$ in $\operatorname T(A)$, its *limit trace* is <a id="r:eq:limit-trace"></a>$$\label{r:eq:limit-trace}
 \lambda([(b_j)])=\lim_{j\to\omega}\bar\tau_j(b_j).$$ Write $\Lambda_\omega$ for exactly this family of traces.

The copies of $A$ and $B$ given by constant sequences are faithful. For $A$, each trace is faithful by simplicity. For $B$, uniform 2-Cauchyness shows that the functions $\tau\mapsto\tau(a_j^*a_j)$ converge uniformly. If all their limits vanish, then $\left\|a_j\right\|_{2,\operatorname T(A)}\to0$, so the class in $B$ is zero. The same observation identifies the constant copy of $B$ in $B^\omega$.

<a id="r:eq:gamma-definition"></a>
We say that $B$ has property $\Gamma$, or equivalently that $A$ has *uniform property $\Gamma$*, if there is a projection $p\in B^\omega\cap B'$ such that $$\label{r:eq:gamma-definition}
 \lambda(px)=\tfrac12\lambda(x)
 \quad(x\in B,\ \lambda\in\Lambda_\omega).$$ This is the tracial-completion formulation of uniform property $\Gamma$; see (Schafhauser, Tikuisis, and White 2026, Definition 16) <a id="pm-cite-37"></a>[[STW](#ref-STW)]. The relation with the standard all-$m$ formulation uses the following interfaces. The framework of (Carrión et al. 2023) <a id="pm-cite-38"></a>[[CCEGSTW](#ref-CCEGSTW)] takes the closed convex hull of $\Lambda_\omega$ as its designated ultrapower trace space. For fixed $p,x$, the identity in <a href="#r:eq:gamma-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="r:eq:gamma-definition">[r:eq:gamma-definition]</a> extends to this closed convex hull by affine weak-star continuity, and the supremum tracial 2-norm is unchanged. The ultrapower identification and transfer of relative commutants are given in (Carrión et al. 2023, Proposition 5.7) <a id="pm-cite-39"></a>[[CCEGSTW](#ref-CCEGSTW)], and (Carrión et al. 2023, Proposition 5.20) <a id="pm-cite-40"></a>[[CCEGSTW](#ref-CCEGSTW)] identifies uniform property $\Gamma$ of $A$ with that of its completion. The passage from a halving projection to equal coefficient-trace partitions is given by (Carrión et al. 2023, Proposition 5.23 and Remark 5.24) <a id="pm-cite-41"></a>[[CCEGSTW](#ref-CCEGSTW)]. Our proof establishes <a href="#r:eq:gamma-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="r:eq:gamma-definition">[r:eq:gamma-definition]</a> directly for the specified completion and the originally fixed ultrafilter.

<a id="r:sec:projections"></a>
### Projection tools

Throughout this section, $D$ is a unital $C^*$-algebra of real rank zero with $\operatorname T(D)\ne\varnothing$. The trace estimates below hold simultaneously for all $\tau\in\operatorname T(D)$. The finite-set construction needs subprojections with prescribed values on finitely many trace tests, followed by a way to move them off previously occupied projections. Scalar compressions and proportional splitting provide the first step; the last lemma controls the trace loss and displacement in the second.

<a id="stmt-r-proj-compress"></a>
<div id="r:proj:compress" class="lemma">

<a id="r:proj:compress-estimate"></a>
**Lemma 6** (Scalar compressions). *Every projection corner $rDr$ has real rank zero. Moreover, given a projection $r\in D$, a finite set $\mathcal F\subset D$, and $\gamma>0$, there is a finite family of nonzero orthogonal projections $q_1,\ldots,q_m$ with sum $r$ such that $$\label{r:proj:compress-estimate}
  \operatorname{dist}(q_j hq_j,\mathbb Cq_j)<\gamma
  \qquad(h\in\mathcal F,\ 1\le j\le m).$$ The family is empty when $r=0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-r-proj-compress"><summary>Proof of stmt-r-proj-compress</summary>

*Proof.* Let $r\ne0$ and $h=h^*\in rDr$. Choose $R>\norm{h}+2$ and finite-spectrum self-adjoints $a_n\in D$ converging in norm to $h+Rr$. The spectrum of the limit is contained in $\{0\}\cup(2,\infty)$, so the spectral projections $f_n=1_{(1,\infty)}(a_n)$ converge in norm to $r$. For all sufficiently large $n$, the element $$r f_n+(1-r)(1-f_n)$$ is invertible. Its unitary polar part $u_n$ satisfies $u_n f_n u_n^*=r$ and $u_n\to1$ in norm. Since $a_n$ commutes with $f_n$, the compression $r u_n a_n u_n^*r$ has finite spectrum in $rDr$. Subtracting $Rr$ gives finite-spectrum approximations to $h$ in that corner.

For the second assertion, first replace $\mathcal F$ by its real and imaginary parts. Compress the first self-adjoint test to $rDr$, approximate it by a finite-spectrum self-adjoint, and partition $r$ by the latter’s spectral projections. Repeat for each subsequent test in every current corner. An estimate $\norm{q hq-\lambda q}<\delta$ persists on every subprojection of $q$. Thus this finite refinement procedure preserves all earlier estimates. Using tolerance less than $\gamma/2$ for the real and imaginary parts proves <a href="#r:proj:compress-estimate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="r:proj:compress-estimate">[r:proj:compress-estimate]</a>; zero pieces are discarded. ◻

</details>

The next fact concerns a simple unital algebra alone. It uses neither real rank zero nor nuclearity nor comparison, and will also supply the small hereditary support for the rank approximation in Section <a href="#u:sec:ranks" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="u:sec:ranks">8</a>.

<a id="stmt-u-lem-thin-full"></a>
<div id="u:lem:thin-full" class="lemma">

**Lemma 7** (Full positive elements with small tracial support). *Let $A$ be simple, unital and infinite dimensional, with $T(A)\ne\varnothing$. For every $\delta>0$ there is a full positive element $e\in A$ with $\norm e=1$ such that $$\tau(\operatorname{supp}(e))<\delta\qquad(\tau\in T(A)).$$ Here supports and traces are taken in each tracial von Neumann representation.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-u-lem-thin-full"><summary>Proof of stmt-u-lem-thin-full</summary>

*Proof.* First we obtain arbitrarily many mutually orthogonal nonzero positive elements. Choose a maximal abelian unital $C^*$-subalgebra $E\subset A$. Suppose that $E$ were finite dimensional, with minimal projections $p_1,\ldots,p_m$ summing to $1$. Every selfadjoint element of $p_iAp_i$ commutes with $E$, so maximality implies $p_iAp_i=\mathbb Cp_i$. If $0\ne x\in p_iAp_j$, then $$x^*x=\norm{x}^2p_j,\qquad xx^*=\norm{x}^2p_i.$$ Thus $u=x/\norm{x}$ satisfies $u^*u=p_j$ and $uu^*=p_i$. For any $y\in p_iAp_j$, the element $u^*y$ is scalar on $p_j$, and $y=u(u^*y)$. Consequently each $p_iAp_j$ has dimension at most one. The decomposition $A=\sum_{i,j}p_iAp_j$ would make $A$ finite dimensional, a contradiction. Hence $E\cong C(X)$ for an infinite compact Hausdorff space $X$. Given $k\in\mathbb N$, choose $k$ distinct points of $X$, pairwise disjoint neighborhoods, and nonzero positive continuous functions supported in those neighborhoods. Their images give mutually orthogonal nonzero positive elements $c_1,\ldots,c_k\in A$.

For two nonzero positive elements $c,d\in A$, simplicity gives $cAd\ne0$. Indeed, if $cAd=0$, then $c$ annihilates the ideal generated by $d$; this ideal is $A$, which would force $c=0$. Choose $y\in A$ such that $z=cyd\ne0$. Then $z^*z\in\overline{dAd}$ and $zz^*\in\overline{cAc}$. In each tracial von Neumann representation, the polar decomposition of $z$ gives a partial isometry with initial projection $\operatorname{supp}(z^*z)$ and final projection $\operatorname{supp}(zz^*)$. The normal tracial state therefore gives $$\tau(\operatorname{supp}(z^*z))=\tau(\operatorname{supp}(zz^*))
 \le \min\{\tau(\operatorname{supp}(c)),\tau(\operatorname{supp}(d))\}
 \qquad(\tau\in T(A)).$$ The elements $y,z$ are chosen independently of $\tau$; the support comparison holds in every tracial representation. Start with $c=c_1$. For $j=2,\ldots,k$, apply the preceding construction with $d=c_j$ and replace $c$ by $zz^*$. The support trace never increases and is at most the support trace of $c_j$. The final nonzero positive element $e=c$ therefore has support trace at most that of each $c_i$, at every trace. Since the support projections of the $c_i$ are orthogonal, $$k\,\tau(\operatorname{supp}(e))\le\sum_{i=1}^k\tau(\operatorname{supp}(c_i))\le1
 \qquad(\tau\in T(A)).$$ Choose $k$ with $1/k<\delta$ and rescale $e$ to have norm one, which does not change its support. It is full because $A$ is simple. ◻

</details>

We return to the ambient algebra $D$. Real rank zero will produce a projection by a spectral cut; the norm-one normalization will let us prove that this projection is full by testing proper quotients of $D$.

<a id="stmt-r-proj-small"></a>
<div id="r:proj:small" class="lemma">

**Lemma 8** (Small full projections). *Suppose that $D$ contains a unital simple infinite-dimensional $C^*$-subalgebra $A$. For every $\delta>0$, there is a full projection $g\in D$ such that $$\sup_{\tau\in\operatorname T(D)}\tau(g)<\delta.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-r-proj-small"><summary>Proof of stmt-r-proj-small</summary>

*Proof.* The restriction to $A$ of any trace in $\operatorname T(D)$ is a tracial state, so $\operatorname T(A)\ne\varnothing$. Given a positive integer $n$, apply Lemma <a href="#u:lem:thin-full" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="u:lem:thin-full">7</a> to obtain $b\in A_+$ with $\norm b=1$ and <a id="r:proj:small-positive"></a>$$\label{r:proj:small-positive}
 \rho(b)\le\rho(\operatorname{supp}(b))<\frac1n
 \qquad(\rho\in\operatorname T(A)).$$

Choose a finite-spectrum self-adjoint $a\in D$ with $\norm{a-b}<1/8$, and set $g=1_{[1/2,\infty)}(a)$. Then $gbg\ge(3/8)g$, so restriction of any $\tau\in\operatorname T(D)$ to $A$ gives <a id="r:proj:small-projection-bound"></a>$$\label{r:proj:small-projection-bound}
  \tau(g)\le\frac83\tau(b)\le\frac{8}{3n}.$$ We claim that $g$ is full in $D$. Otherwise, let $\pi:D\to D/I$ be the quotient by the proper closed ideal generated by $g$. The inequalities $a\ge-1/8$ and $a(1-g)\le(1/2)(1-g)$ imply $\norm{\pi(a)}\le1/2$, and hence $\norm{\pi(b)}\le5/8$. On the other hand, $\pi|_A$ is a unital homomorphism from the simple algebra $A$ to a nonzero unital algebra. It is injective and therefore isometric, contradicting $\norm{b}=1$. Thus $g$ is full, and choosing $n$ with $8/(3n)<\delta$ finishes the proof. ◻

</details>

We will use an elementary splitting procedure with different target projections. Zero projections are allowed among the resulting pieces.

<a id="stmt-r-proj-columns"></a>
<div id="r:proj:columns" class="lemma">

**Lemma 9** (Splitting a column). *Let $f,g_1,\ldots,g_m$ be projections in $D$, where $m\ge1$. Suppose that $v_1,\ldots,v_m\in D$ satisfy $$v_i=g_i v_i f,
  \qquad \sum_{i=1}^m v_i^*v_i=f.$$ There are orthogonal projections $e_1,\ldots,e_m$ with sum $f$ and $e_i\precsim g_i$ for every $i$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-r-proj-columns"><summary>Proof of stmt-r-proj-columns</summary>

*Proof.* If $f=0$, take all $e_i=0$. We argue by induction on $m$; for $m=1$, $v_1$ itself implements $f\precsim g_1$. Suppose that $m>1$. Apply Lemma <a href="#r:proj:compress" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="r:proj:compress">6</a> to choose a finite-spectrum self-adjoint $a\in fDf$ within $1/8$ of $v_1^*v_1$. Set $$e_1=1_{[1/2,\infty)}(a),\qquad r=f-e_1.$$ The inequality $e_1v_1^*v_1e_1\ge(3/8)e_1$ shows that, when $e_1\ne0$, $$v_1e_1(e_1v_1^*v_1e_1)^{-1/2}$$ is a partial isometry with initial projection $e_1$ and final projection below $g_1$. On the remaining corner, $$Q:=\sum_{i=2}^m r v_i^*v_i r
    =r-rv_1^*v_1r\ge\frac38r.$$ If $r=0$, take $e_2=\cdots=e_m=0$. Otherwise define $w_i=v_i r Q^{-1/2}$ for $i=2,\ldots,m$, with the inverse taken in $rDr$. These elements satisfy $w_i=g_iw_i r$ and $\sum_{i=2}^m w_i^*w_i=r$. The induction hypothesis partitions $r$ as required. ◻

</details>

Approximate projection halving in non-elementary simple real-rank-zero algebras was developed by Zhang (Zhang 1991, Theorem 1.1(i)) <a id="pm-cite-42"></a>[[Zhang1991](#ref-Zhang1991)]. More directly, Zhang’s ordered-decomposition lemma (Zhang 1990, Lemma 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-43"></a>[[Zhang1990](#ref-Zhang1990)], as used in Vaccaro’s divisibility argument (Vaccaro 2026, Lemma 2.1, version 1) <a id="pm-cite-44"></a>[[Vac26](#ref-Vac26)], applies without simplicity: a projection can be partitioned into finitely many pieces ordered by subequivalence and each subequivalent to any given full projection. We give this projection construction below and then use a common rounding rule to obtain proportional trace splitting uniformly over all traces.

<a id="stmt-r-proj-split"></a>
<div id="r:proj:split" class="proposition">

<a id="r:proj:split-estimate"></a>
**Proposition 10** (Uniform proportional subprojections). *Assume that for every $\delta>0$ there is a full projection $g\in D$ with $\sup_{\tau\in\operatorname T(D)}\tau(g)<\delta$. For every projection $f\in D$, every $s\in[0,1]$, and every $\varepsilon>0$, there is a projection $e\le f$ such that $$\label{r:proj:split-estimate}
  \sup_{\tau\in\operatorname T(D)}|\tau(e)-s\tau(f)|<\varepsilon.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-r-proj-split"><summary>Proof of stmt-r-proj-split</summary>

*Proof.* The case $f=0$ is immediate. Choose a full projection $g$ with $\sup_\tau\tau(g)<\varepsilon$. Fullness provides a finite sum $\sum_i x_i g y_i$ within distance less than one of $1$. Write $X=[x_1\ \cdots\ x_m]$ and let $Y$ be the column with entries $gy_i$. Then $XY$ is invertible, so $Y$ has a bounded left inverse $L=(XY)^{-1}X$. In particular, $$1=Y^*L^*LY\le\norm{L}^2Y^*Y,$$ and $Y^*Y$ is invertible. The entries of $Y(Y^*Y)^{-1/2}f$ form a column as in Lemma <a href="#r:proj:columns" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="r:proj:columns">9</a>, with every target projection equal to $g$. We obtain an orthogonal family with sum $f$, each member subequivalent to $g$.

<a id="r:proj:ordered-partition"></a>
We next replace this family by one, say $s_1,\ldots,s_m$, satisfying $$\label{r:proj:ordered-partition}
  s_1\precsim s_2\precsim\cdots\precsim s_m\precsim g.$$ This ordering makes the trace weights increase in the same order for every trace, so that one choice of rounding coefficients will work simultaneously for all of them. Here is the pair operation that makes this possible. Suppose that $x,y$ are orthogonal pieces, both subequivalent to a common projection $h$. Choose partial isometries $w,u$ with $$w^*w=x,\quad ww^*=x'\le h,
  \qquad u^*u=y,\quad uu^*\le h.$$ Since $u^*x'u+u^*(h-x')u=y$, Lemma <a href="#r:proj:columns" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="r:proj:columns">9</a> applied to the column with entries $x'u,(h-x')u$ gives $y=k+l$ and partial isometries $\alpha,\beta$ with $$\alpha^*\alpha=k,\quad \alpha\alpha^*\le x',
  \qquad \beta^*\beta=l,\quad \beta\beta^*\le h-x'.$$ Set $\xi=w^*\alpha+l$ and $\zeta=w+\beta$. Then $$\begin{aligned}
  \xi^*\xi&=y,&
  \xi\xi^*&=w^*\alpha\alpha^*w+l\le x+l,\\
  \zeta^*\zeta&=x+l,&
  \zeta\zeta^*&=x'+\beta\beta^*\le h.
\end{aligned}$$ The cross terms vanish by orthogonality of the initial and final components: $x\perp l$ and $x'\perp(h-x')$. Thus replacing $x,y$ by $k,x+l$ preserves their sum and orthogonality, and $$k\precsim x\le x+l,
  \qquad x,y\precsim x+l\precsim h.$$ After each adjacent comparison, the new right-hand piece dominates every piece already passed: it contains the previous moving piece. Thus a left-to-right pass with common bound $g$ leaves a last piece dominating all earlier pieces. Freeze that last piece and repeat on the shorter list, now using it as the common bound. Induction gives <a href="#r:proj:ordered-partition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="r:proj:ordered-partition">[r:proj:ordered-partition]</a>. All operations take place inside $D$ and preserve the orthogonal sum $f$.

Set $$c_i=\lfloor si\rfloor-\lfloor s(i-1)\rfloor\in\{0,1\},
  \qquad e=\sum_{i=1}^m c_i s_i.$$ For a fixed $\tau\in\operatorname T(D)$, put $t_i=\tau(s_i)$ and $D_j=\lfloor sj\rfloor-sj$. Then $t_1\le\cdots\le t_m\le\tau(g)$ and $-1\le D_j\le0$. Summation by parts gives $$\tau(e)-s\tau(f)
   =D_m t_m+\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}D_i(t_i-t_{i+1}).$$ The first term lies in $[-t_m,0]$, while the sum lies in $[0,t_m-t_1]$. It follows that $|\tau(e)-s\tau(f)|\le t_m\le\tau(g)$, proving <a href="#r:proj:split-estimate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="r:proj:split-estimate">[r:proj:split-estimate]</a> uniformly in $\tau$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-r-proj-tests"></a>
<div id="r:proj:tests" class="corollary">

**Corollary 11** (Splitting finite linear tests). *Under the small-full-projection hypothesis of Proposition <a href="#r:proj:split" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="r:proj:split">10</a>, let $q\in D$ be a projection, $s\in[0,1]$, and $\mathcal F\subset D$ a finite set. For every $\varepsilon>0$, there is a projection $e\le q$ such that <a id="r:proj:tests-estimate"></a>$$\label{r:proj:tests-estimate}
  |\tau(eh)-s\tau(qh)|<\varepsilon
  \qquad(h\in\mathcal F,\ \tau\in\operatorname T(D)).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-r-proj-tests"><summary>Proof of stmt-r-proj-tests</summary>

*Proof.* The assertion is immediate if $q=0$ or $\mathcal F$ is empty. Choose $\gamma>0$ with $2\gamma<\varepsilon/2$ and use Lemma <a href="#r:proj:compress" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="r:proj:compress">6</a> to write $q=\sum_{j=1}^m q_j$ with $$\norm{q_j hq_j-\lambda_{j,h}q_j}<\gamma
  \qquad(h\in\mathcal F)$$ for suitable scalars $\lambda_{j,h}$. These scalars satisfy $|\lambda_{j,h}|\le\norm{h}+\gamma$. Put $M=\max_{h\in\mathcal F}\norm{h}+\gamma$ and choose $\delta>0$ with $mM\delta<\varepsilon/2$. By Proposition <a href="#r:proj:split" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="r:proj:split">10</a>, choose $e_j\le q_j$ so that $|\tau(e_j)-s\tau(q_j)|<\delta$ for every $\tau$, and set $e=\sum_j e_j$. Traciality removes all off-diagonal compressions and gives $$\begin{aligned}
  |\tau(eh)-s\tau(qh)|
  &\le \gamma\bigl(\tau(e)+s\tau(q)\bigr)
       +\sum_{j=1}^m |\lambda_{j,h}|
          |\tau(e_j)-s\tau(q_j)| \\
  &\le 2\gamma+mM\delta<\varepsilon.
\end{aligned}$$ ◻

</details>

The last tool moves a projection off an occupied corner. Its linear trace estimate retains the scale of the projection being moved.

<a id="stmt-r-proj-move"></a>
<div id="r:proj:move" class="lemma">

<a id="r:proj:move-mass"></a>
<a id="r:proj:move-distance"></a>
<a id="r:proj:move-linear"></a>
**Lemma 12** (Orthogonal insertion). *For projections $z,y\in D$, there is a projection $t\le1-z$ such that $$\begin{aligned}
  \tau(y)-3\tau(yz)&\le\tau(t)\le\tau(y),
       \label{r:proj:move-mass}\\
  \left\|t-y\right\|_{2,\tau}^{2}&\le5\tau(yz)
       \label{r:proj:move-distance}
\end{aligned}$$ for every $\tau\in\operatorname T(D)$. For every contraction $x\in D$, it also satisfies $$\label{r:proj:move-linear}
  |\tau((t-y)x)|
   \le2\sqrt{\tau(y)}\,\left\|t-y\right\|_{2,\tau}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-r-proj-move"><summary>Proof of stmt-r-proj-move</summary>

<a id="r:proj:discarded-mass"></a>
*Proof.* If $y=0$, take $t=0$. Otherwise choose, in $yDy$, a finite-spectrum self-adjoint $a$ with $\norm{a-yzy}<1/8$, and let $f=1_{(-\infty,1/2)}(a)$. Writing $d=y-f$, we have $$d(yzy)d\ge\frac38d,
  \qquad f(yzy)f\le\frac58f.$$ Since $yzy\ge0$, this implies $$\label{r:proj:discarded-mass}
  \tau(y-f)\le\frac83\tau(yz),
  \qquad A_f:=f(1-z)f\ge\frac38f.$$ If $f=0$, put $t=0$. Otherwise the element $$v=(1-z)f A_f^{-1/2}$$ is a partial isometry with $v^*v=f$; set $t=vv^*\le1-z$. In both cases, $$\tau(t)=\tau(f),\qquad ftf=f(1-z)f.$$ The mass bounds <a href="#r:proj:move-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="r:proj:move-mass">[r:proj:move-mass]</a> follow from <a href="#r:proj:discarded-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="r:proj:discarded-mass">[r:proj:discarded-mass]</a>. As $f\le y$, $$\tau(ty)\ge\tau(tf)=\tau(f)-\tau(fz).$$ Consequently, $$\begin{aligned}
  \left\|t-y\right\|_{2,\tau}^{2}
   &=\tau(t)+\tau(y)-2\tau(ty)\\
   &\le\tau(y-f)+2\tau(fz)
    \le\left(\frac83+2\right)\tau(yz)
    \le5\tau(yz),
\end{aligned}$$ which proves <a href="#r:proj:move-distance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="r:proj:move-distance">[r:proj:move-distance]</a>.

Finally, decompose $t-y=t(t-y)+(1-t)(t-y)$. The first summand has left support below $t$, and the second equals $-(1-t)y$ and has right support below $y$. Tracial Cauchy–Schwarz therefore gives $$\left\|t-y\right\|_{1,\tau}
   \le\bigl(\sqrt{\tau(t)}+\sqrt{\tau(y)}\bigr)
       \left\|t-y\right\|_{2,\tau}
   \le2\sqrt{\tau(y)}\,\left\|t-y\right\|_{2,\tau}.$$ Trace-norm duality proves <a href="#r:proj:move-linear" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="r:proj:move-linear">[r:proj:move-linear]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="r:sec:weighted"></a>
### Nuclear averaging and weighted matrix blocks

In this section $A\subseteq D$ is a unital inclusion, $A$ is nuclear, and $D$ has real rank zero and nonempty tracial state space. For a prescribed finite set of unitary tests, nuclear averaging turns a projection into a nearly central positive contraction. We will approximate this contraction in trace by a weighted sum of projections, keeping their total weighted squared commutator norm small. Each projection will also be represented as a matrix block over a projection $q$, with each compressed test close to a scalar matrix over $q$. This lets us sample a subprojection $e\leq q$ simultaneously in the matrix coordinates while retaining trace and commutator estimates, without requiring $e$ to be central. We first choose the averaging map, independently of its input projection.

<a id="stmt-r-weighted-diagonal"></a>
<div id="r:weighted:diagonal" class="lemma">

<a id="r:eq:diagonal-mass"></a>
<a id="r:eq:diagonal-central"></a>
**Lemma 13** (Averaging with trace control). *For a finite $K\subseteq\mathcal U(A)$ and $\mu>0$, there are contractions $v_1,\ldots,v_m\in A$ and numbers $\lambda_l\geq0$ with $\sum_l\lambda_l=1$ such that the map $$\Phi(b)=\sum_{l=1}^m\lambda_l v_lbv_l^*,\qquad b\in D,$$ satisfies, for every projection $q\in D$, every $x\in K$, and every $\tau\in\operatorname T(D)$, $$\begin{aligned}
 0\leq\Phi(q)&\leq1,\qquad
 \tau(q)-\mu\leq\tau(\Phi(q))\leq\tau(q),\label{r:eq:diagonal-mass}\\
 \left\|\Phi(q)-x\Phi(q)x^*\right\|_{1,\tau}
 &\leq\mu\tau(q).\label{r:eq:diagonal-central}
\end{aligned}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-r-weighted-diagonal"><summary>Proof of stmt-r-weighted-diagonal</summary>

<a id="r:eq:approx-diagonal"></a>
*Proof.* Let $E=A\widehat\otimes A$ denote the Banach projective tensor product, with norm $\norm{\cdot}_\pi$, multiplication map $m_A(a\otimes b)=ab$, and bimodule actions $x\cdot(a\otimes b)=xa\otimes b$ and $(a\otimes b)\cdot x=a\otimes bx$. Haagerup’s virtual diagonal theorem (Haagerup 1983, Theorem 3.1) <a id="pm-cite-45"></a>[[Haa83](#ref-Haa83)] gives a virtual diagonal $M\in E^{**}$ in the weak-star closed convex hull of $\{v\otimes v^*:v\in A,\ \norm v\leq1\}$. Its bimodule commutators vanish and $m_A^{**}(M)=1$. Apply the second adjoint of the bounded linear map $$T:E\longrightarrow E^K\oplus A,\qquad
 T(d)=\big((x\cdot d-d\cdot x)_{x\in K},m_A(d)\big).$$ Its value at $M$ is $(0,\ldots,0,1)$, in the canonical copy of $E^K\oplus A$. Weak-star convergence in the bidual restricts to weak convergence in this copy, so this point lies in the weak closure of the image of the finite convex hull. A convex subset of a Banach space has the same weak and norm closures, by Hahn–Banach separation. We may therefore choose a finite convex combination $d=\sum_l\lambda_l v_l\otimes v_l^*$ such that $$\label{r:eq:approx-diagonal}
 \norm{x\cdot d-d\cdot x}_\pi<\mu\quad(x\in K),\qquad
 \norm{1-\sum_l\lambda_l v_lv_l^*}<\mu.$$

Both sums $\sum_l\lambda_l v_lv_l^*$ and $\sum_l\lambda_l v_l^*v_l$ are positive contractions. By traciality, the positive element $c=1-\sum_l\lambda_l v_l^*v_l$ satisfies $0\leq\tau(c)<\mu$. Since $\tau(q)-\tau(\Phi(q))=\tau(qc)$, this proves <a href="#r:eq:diagonal-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="r:eq:diagonal-mass">[r:eq:diagonal-mass]</a>. For the centrality estimate, the map $a\otimes b\mapsto aqb$ has norm at most $\tau(q)$ from the projective tensor norm to the tracial 1-seminorm: indeed, $$\left\|aqb\right\|_{1,\tau}
 \leq\left\|aq\right\|_{2,\tau}\left\|qb\right\|_{2,\tau}
 \leq\norm a\norm b\,\tau(q).$$ Apply this map to the first inequality in <a href="#r:eq:approx-diagonal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="r:eq:approx-diagonal">[r:eq:approx-diagonal]</a> and multiply by the unitary $x^*$. ◻

</details>

The following tracial square-root estimate is the form of the Powers–Størmer inequality needed below. Their original (Powers and Størmer 1970, Lemma 4.1) <a id="pm-cite-46"></a>[[PowersStormer1970](#ref-PowersStormer1970)] concerns positive Hilbert-space operators with the ordinary trace; the short proof here gives the arbitrary tracial $C^*$-algebra version directly.

<a id="stmt-r-weighted-square-root"></a>
<div id="r:weighted:square-root" class="lemma">

**Lemma 14** (A square-root trace estimate). *For positive $b,c\in D$ and $\tau\in\operatorname T(D)$, $$\left\|\sqrt b-\sqrt c\right\|_{2,\tau}^2
 \leq\left\|b-c\right\|_{1,\tau}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-r-weighted-square-root"><summary>Proof of stmt-r-weighted-square-root</summary>

*Proof.* Work in the tracial von Neumann algebra and put $d=\sqrt b-\sqrt c$. Traciality and $b-c=\sqrt b\,d+d\sqrt c$ give $$\tau(\mathop{\mathrm{sgn}}(d)(b-c))=\tau((\sqrt b+\sqrt c)|d|).$$ Since $\sqrt b+\sqrt c\geq d$ and $\sqrt b+\sqrt c\geq-d$, tracing against the positive and negative parts $d_+$ and $d_-$, respectively, bounds the right-hand side below by $\tau(d_+^2)+\tau(d_-^2)=\tau(d^2)$. Trace-norm duality bounds the left-hand side by $\left\|b-c\right\|_{1,\tau}$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-r-weighted-family"></a>
<div id="r:weighted:family" class="proposition">

<a id="r:eq:block-isometry"></a>
<a id="r:eq:block-compression"></a>
<a id="r:eq:block-mass"></a>
<a id="r:eq:block-energy"></a>
**Proposition 15** (Weighted matrix blocks). *Let $K\subseteq\mathcal U(A)$ be finite and $0<\eta<1$. There are contractions $v_1,\ldots,v_m\in A$ and nonnegative numbers $\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_m$ summing to one, chosen using only $K,\eta$, such that the map <a id="r:eq:averaging-map"></a>$$\label{r:eq:averaging-map}
 \Phi:D\longrightarrow D,\qquad
 \Phi(b)=\sum_{l=1}^m\lambda_l v_lbv_l^*,$$ satisfies <a href="#r:eq:diagonal-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="r:eq:diagonal-mass">[r:eq:diagonal-mass]</a> with $\mu=\eta^2$. For every projection $r\in D$, there is a finite family consisting of weights $0<w_j\leq1$, nonzero projections $q_j\leq r$, integers $k_j\geq1$, and rows $U_j\in M_{1,k_j}(D)$, such that, with $p_j=U_jU_j^*$, $$\begin{aligned}
 U_j^*U_j&=1_{k_j}\otimes q_j,\label{r:eq:block-isometry}\\
 \operatorname{dist}\big(U_j^*xU_j,M_{k_j}(\mathbb C)\otimes q_j\big)&<\eta
       &&(x\in K),\label{r:eq:block-compression}\\
 \left\|\sum_jw_jp_j-\Phi(r)\right\|_{1,\tau}&\leq\eta^2,
       \label{r:eq:block-mass}\\
 \sum_jw_j\left\|[x,p_j]\right\|_{2,\tau}^2&\leq6\eta
       &&(x\in K).\label{r:eq:block-energy}
\end{aligned}$$ The last two estimates hold for every $\tau\in\operatorname T(D)$. The family may be empty; the base projections $q_j$ may repeat, and the projections $p_j$ need not be mutually orthogonal.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-r-weighted-family"><summary>Proof of stmt-r-weighted-family</summary>

*Proof.* Put $\mu=\eta^2$ and choose $\Phi$ by Lemma <a href="#r:weighted:diagonal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="r:weighted:diagonal">13</a>. In particular, the coefficient list is fixed before $r$. Choose $0<\sigma<1$, then $\beta>0$, so that <a id="r:eq:block-parameters"></a>$$\label{r:eq:block-parameters}
 \beta<\sigma/2,\qquad m(\sigma+2\beta)<\mu,\qquad
 \frac{2\beta}{\sigma}
       +\frac{(2+\sqrt2)\beta}{\sigma^2}<\eta.$$ This is possible by choosing $\sigma$ with $m\sigma<\mu$ first.

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
###### Scalar matrix approximation.

<a id="r:eq:scalar-gram"></a>
Partition $r$ by Lemma <a href="#r:proj:compress" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="r:proj:compress">6</a>, using the finite list $$v_l^*v_n,\quad v_l^*xv_n
 \qquad(1\leq l,n\leq m,\ x\in K).$$ Take the compression tolerance smaller than $\beta/(2m)$. For a nonzero partition piece $q$, set $$V=[\sqrt{\lambda_l}\,v_lq]_{l=1}^m,\quad
 h_q=VV^*=\Phi(q),\quad M=V^*V,\quad N_x=V^*xV.$$ These are contractions in their respective matrix spaces. Apply any state of $qDq$ entrywise to $M,N_x$, obtaining scalar matrices $G,H_x$. Positivity and contractivity of a state at matrix levels give $0\leq G\leq1$ and $\norm{H_x}\leq1$. The compression tolerance implies $$\label{r:eq:scalar-gram}
 \norm{M-G\otimes q}<\beta,\qquad
 \norm{N_x-H_x\otimes q}<\beta.$$ Indeed, each unweighted entry differs from its scalar state value by less than twice the compression tolerance, and an $m$-by-$m$ matrix of entries of norm at most $c$ has norm at most $mc$.

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
###### Construction of the blocks.

<a id="r:eq:finite-spectrum-close"></a>
Rotate the columns of $V$ by a scalar unitary diagonalizing $G$. Retain the columns corresponding to eigenvalues at least $\sigma$, forming a row $W$ with $k$ columns and scalar diagonal matrix $G_+$. The cutoff gives the uniform lower bound needed to normalize the retained row by an inverse square root. If $k=0$, set $b_q=0$. Otherwise put <a id="r:eq:polar-block"></a>$$\label{r:eq:polar-block}
 X=W^*W\geq(\sigma/2)(1_k\otimes q),\qquad
 U=WX^{-1/2},\qquad b_q=U(G_+\otimes q)U^*.$$ Then $U^*U=1_k\otimes q$, so $b_q$ is a finite-spectrum positive contraction. Writing $W_-$ for the row of deleted columns, we have $\norm{W_-^*W_-}\leq\sigma+\beta$, and therefore $$\tau(W_-W_-^*)=(\operatorname{Tr}\otimes\tau)(W_-^*W_-)
 \leq m(\sigma+\beta)\tau(q).$$ The contribution is zero if no columns are deleted. Also $$b_q-WW^*=U(G_+\otimes q-X)U^*$$ has norm at most $\beta$, supported on a projection of trace $k\tau(q)$. Consequently, including the case $k=0$, $$\label{r:eq:finite-spectrum-close}
 \left\|b_q-h_q\right\|_{1,\tau}
 \leq m(\sigma+2\beta)\tau(q)\leq\mu\tau(q).$$

<a id="r:eq:inverse-root"></a>
We verify explicitly that the compression estimate is independent of the corner and its matrix size. For positive invertible $X,Y\geq a1$, the resolvent formula for inverse square roots gives $$\label{r:eq:inverse-root}
 \norm{X^{-1/2}-Y^{-1/2}}
 \leq\frac{\norm{X-Y}}{\pi}
       \int_0^\infty\frac{t^{-1/2}}{(a+t)^2}\,dt
 =\frac{\norm{X-Y}}{2a^{3/2}}.$$ Use $Y=G_+\otimes q$ and $a=\sigma/2$. If $H\otimes q$ denotes the corresponding retained corner of $H_x\otimes q$, then $\norm H\leq1$, and <a href="#r:eq:scalar-gram" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="r:eq:scalar-gram">[r:eq:scalar-gram]</a> gives $\norm{W^*xW-H\otimes q}<\beta$. Expanding the difference between $$U^*xU=X^{-1/2}(W^*xW)X^{-1/2}
 \quad\hbox{and}\quad Y^{-1/2}(H\otimes q)Y^{-1/2}$$ and using <a href="#r:eq:inverse-root" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="r:eq:inverse-root">[r:eq:inverse-root]</a> bounds its norm by $$\frac{2\beta}{\sigma}
 +\frac{(2+\sqrt2)\beta}{\sigma^2}<\eta.$$ The second expression is a scalar matrix over $q$. The tolerances $\sigma,\beta$ were chosen before the partition of $r$, so the compression bound is uniform over its pieces.

Write $b_q$ as the finite sum of its nonzero upper spectral projections, weighted by the lengths of their level intervals: <a id="r:eq:layer-cake"></a>$$\label{r:eq:layer-cake}
 b_q=\int_0^1 1_{(a,\infty)}(b_q)\,da
     =\sum_{j\in J_q}w_jp_j.$$ Each $p_j$ is the image of a scalar coordinate projection under $U$. It therefore has a subrow $U_j$ satisfying <a href="#r:eq:block-isometry" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="r:eq:block-isometry">[r:eq:block-isometry]</a>, with $q_j=q$. Taking matrix corners gives <a href="#r:eq:block-compression" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="r:eq:block-compression">[r:eq:block-compression]</a>. Summing <a href="#r:eq:finite-spectrum-close" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="r:eq:finite-spectrum-close">[r:eq:finite-spectrum-close]</a> over the partition gives <a href="#r:eq:block-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="r:eq:block-mass">[r:eq:block-mass]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
###### Averaged commutators.

For a finite-spectrum positive contraction $b=\sum_s s e_s$, include its kernel projection in this spectral resolution. Tracial orthogonality gives, for unitary $x$, <a id="r:eq:spectral-energy"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \int_0^1\left\|[x,1_{(a,\infty)}(b)]\right\|_{2,\tau}^2\,da
 &=\sum_{s,t}|s-t|\left\|e_sxe_t\right\|_{2,\tau}^2\notag\\
 &\leq2\sqrt{\tau(b)}\,\left\|[x,\sqrt b]\right\|_{2,\tau}\notag\\
 &\leq2\sqrt{\tau(b)\left\|b-xbx^*\right\|_{1,\tau}}.
 \label{r:eq:spectral-energy}
\end{aligned}$$ For the middle inequality, factor $|s-t|=|\sqrt s-\sqrt t|(\sqrt s+\sqrt t)$ and apply Cauchy–Schwarz to the sum. The second squared sum is at most $2\sum_{s,t}(s+t)\left\|e_sxe_t\right\|_{2,\tau}^2=4\tau(b)$. The last inequality is Lemma <a href="#r:weighted:square-root" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="r:weighted:square-root">14</a>.

For $b=b_q$, equations <a href="#r:eq:diagonal-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="r:eq:diagonal-mass">[r:eq:diagonal-mass]</a>, <a href="#r:eq:diagonal-central" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="r:eq:diagonal-central">[r:eq:diagonal-central]</a>, and <a href="#r:eq:finite-spectrum-close" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="r:eq:finite-spectrum-close">[r:eq:finite-spectrum-close]</a> give $$\tau(b_q)\leq(1+\mu)\tau(q),\qquad
 \left\|b_q-xb_qx^*\right\|_{1,\tau}\leq3\mu\tau(q).$$ Thus the contribution of $J_q$ in <a href="#r:eq:block-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="r:eq:block-energy">[r:eq:block-energy]</a> is at most $2\sqrt{3(1+\mu)}\,\eta\tau(q)\leq6\eta\tau(q)$. Summing over $q$ proves the assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="r:sec:packing"></a>
### Sampling and orthogonal packing

Continue with the inclusion $A\subseteq D$ from Section <a href="#r:sec:weighted" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="r:sec:weighted">4</a>. The matrix compressions from Proposition <a href="#r:weighted:family" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="r:weighted:family">15</a> allow us to sample a projection without making the sampled corner central. We isolate the precise estimate before carrying out the packing.

<a id="stmt-r-packing-sampling"></a>
<div id="r:packing:sampling" class="lemma">

<a id="r:eq:sampling-linear"></a>
<a id="r:eq:sampling-energy"></a>
**Lemma 16** (Sampling a matrix block). *Let $q\in D$ be a nonzero projection and let $U\in M_{1,k}(D)$ satisfy $U^*U=1_k\otimes q$. Put $p=UU^*$. Suppose $x\in\mathcal U(D)$, $0<\eta<1$, and $$\operatorname{dist}(U^*xU,M_k(\mathbb C)\otimes q)<\eta.$$ For a projection $e\leq q$, set $y=U(1_k\otimes e)U^*$. Then, for $0\leq c\leq1$, every $\tau\in\operatorname T(D)$, and $\delta_\tau=|\tau(y)-c\tau(p)|$, $$\begin{aligned}
 |\tau(yx)-c\tau(px)|
 &\leq\eta\big(\tau(y)+c\tau(p)\big)+2\delta_\tau,
 \label{r:eq:sampling-linear}\\
 \left|\left\|[x,y]\right\|_{2,\tau}^2
        -c\left\|[x,p]\right\|_{2,\tau}^2\right|
 &\leq6\eta\big(\tau(y)+c\tau(p)\big)+10\delta_\tau.
 \label{r:eq:sampling-energy}
\end{aligned}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-r-packing-sampling"><summary>Proof of stmt-r-packing-sampling</summary>

*Proof.* Write $T=U^*xU$, $P_e=1_k\otimes e$, and choose $T_0\in M_k(\mathbb C)$ with $\norm{T-T_0\otimes q}<\eta$. Since $T$ is a contraction, $\norm{T_0}\leq2$. The map $a\mapsto UaU^*$ identifies the relevant matrix corner with its image in $D$, and therefore $$\tau(yx)=(\operatorname{Tr}\otimes\tau)(P_eTP_e),\qquad
 \tau(|yxy|^2)=(\operatorname{Tr}\otimes\tau)(|P_eTP_e|^2).$$ The scalar matrix $T_0\otimes q$ commutes with $P_e$, whether or not $e$ commutes with any entry of $T$. Thus replacing $P_eTP_e$ by $T_0\otimes e$ changes these two traces by at most $\eta\tau(y)$ and $3\eta\tau(y)$, respectively. For the latter estimate use $$\norm{R^*R-S^*S}\leq(\norm R+\norm S)\norm{R-S},
 \quad R=P_eTP_e,\quad S=T_0\otimes e,$$ and the fact that the trace of the corner unit is $(\operatorname{Tr}\otimes\tau)(P_e)=k\tau(e)=\tau(y)$. The same estimates hold with $e$ replaced by $q$.

<a id="r:eq:unitary-energy"></a>
Set $$\alpha=\frac{\operatorname{Tr}(T_0)}{k},\qquad
 \beta_0=\frac{\operatorname{Tr}(T_0^*T_0)}{k}.$$ Then $|\alpha|\leq2$ and $0\leq\beta_0\leq4$. The scalar reference traces for $y$ are $\alpha\tau(y)$ and $\beta_0\tau(y)$; for $p$, they are $\alpha\tau(p)$ and $\beta_0\tau(p)$. This proves <a href="#r:eq:sampling-linear" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="r:eq:sampling-linear">[r:eq:sampling-linear]</a>. For the second estimate, the exact identity $$\label{r:eq:unitary-energy}
 \left\|[x,f]\right\|_{2,\tau}^2
 =2\tau(f)-2\tau(|fxf|^2)
 \qquad(f\text{ a projection},\ x\text{ unitary})$$ follows by expansion and traciality. Applying it to $y,p$ and using the squared compression estimates proves <a href="#r:eq:sampling-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="r:eq:sampling-energy">[r:eq:sampling-energy]</a>; the mass-discrepancy coefficient is bounded by $2+2\beta_0\leq10$. In particular, <a href="#r:eq:unitary-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="r:eq:unitary-energy">[r:eq:unitary-energy]</a> also accounts for the part of $x$ that leaves the larger block $p$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-r-packing-orthogonal"></a>
<div id="r:packing:orthogonal" class="lemma">

**Lemma 17** (Energy of orthogonal sums). *If $t_1,\ldots,t_M$ are mutually orthogonal projections and $z=\sum_a t_a$, then for $x\in D$ and $\tau\in\operatorname T(D)$, $$\left\|[x,z]\right\|_{2,\tau}^2
 \leq\sum_a\left\|[x,t_a]\right\|_{2,\tau}^2.$$ The same bound holds for a sum over any subfamily.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-r-packing-orthogonal"><summary>Proof of stmt-r-packing-orthogonal</summary>

*Proof.* The two off-diagonal corners of the commutator are orthogonal in the tracial 2-seminorm, so its square is $$\left\|(1-z)xz\right\|_{2,\tau}^2+\left\|zx(1-z)\right\|_{2,\tau}^2.$$ Splitting the $z$-corner into the orthogonal $t_a$’s writes each term as a sum of squared norms. Since $1-z\leq1-t_a$, the respective summands are bounded by $\left\|(1-t_a)xt_a\right\|_{2,\tau}^2$ and $\left\|t_ax(1-t_a)\right\|_{2,\tau}^2$. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-r-packing-orthogonal-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-r-packing-orthogonal</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#r:thm:finite" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="r:thm:finite">3</a>.* Fix a finite $K\subseteq\mathcal U(A)$. We construct orthogonal projections $P,S$ such that $P$ nearly commutes with $K$, their coefficient traces nearly agree, and $P+S$ nearly fills the unit in trace. The identity $$2\tau(Px)-\tau(x)
 =\tau((P-S)x)-\tau((1-P-S)x)
 \qquad(x\in K)$$ then gives the required halving estimate. We obtain $P,S$ as two labelled sums, sampling equal proportions for the two signs in each round and moving the samples into the unoccupied corner. We use $H>0$ and $N\in\mathbb N$, with $\eta=H/N<1$. First $H$ is chosen to reduce the unoccupied mass; then $N$ is increased at fixed $H$ to reduce the errors.

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
###### Preparing the averaging maps.

When an averaging map acts on the remaining corner, the projections inserted earlier must already approximately commute with its coefficient contractions. Since the map is chosen independently of its input projection, we can arrange these coefficient tests in advance, working backwards through the rounds. Construct finite sets of unitaries $$K_1\supseteq K_2\supseteq\cdots\supseteq K_N=K$$ backward. Having chosen $K_i$, choose the map $\Phi_i$ from Proposition <a href="#r:weighted:family" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="r:weighted:family">15</a> for $K_i,\eta$. If $i>1$, express each of its coefficient contractions $v_l$ as a linear combination of unitaries with coefficient absolute sum at most two, and include these unitaries, together with $K_i$, in $K_{i-1}$.

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
###### The induction.

<a id="r:eq:energy-recurrence"></a>
<a id="r:eq:energy-induction"></a>
We insert finitely many projections in each of $N$ rounds. All inserted projections will be mutually orthogonal and each will carry a label $+$ or $-$. Let $Z_i$ be their cumulative sum after round $i$, with $Z_0=0$. Set $$\label{r:eq:energy-recurrence}
 E_0=0,\qquad E_i-E_{i-1}=1000\eta(E_{i-1}+\eta).$$ We maintain the bound $$\label{r:eq:energy-induction}
 \sum_{t\text{ inserted through round }i}
       \left\|[x,t]\right\|_{2,\tau}^2\leq E_i
 \quad(x\in K_i,\ \tau\in\operatorname T(D)).$$ In particular, Lemma <a href="#r:packing:orthogonal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="r:packing:orthogonal">17</a> gives the same bound for the squared commutator of any subfamily sum.

<a id="r:eq:old-overlap"></a>
In round $i$, let $r=1-Z_{i-1}$ and choose the weighted family from Proposition <a href="#r:weighted:family" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="r:weighted:family">15</a> for $\Phi_i,r$. For every $\tau\in\operatorname T(D)$, $$\label{r:eq:old-overlap}
 \tau(Z_{i-1}\Phi_i(r))
 =\sum_l\lambda_l\left\|Z_{i-1}v_lr\right\|_{2,\tau}^2
 \leq4E_{i-1}.$$ For $i=1$ the left-hand side is zero. Otherwise each unitary in the chosen decomposition of $v_l$ lies in $K_{i-1}$, so the induction and Lemma <a href="#r:packing:orthogonal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="r:packing:orthogonal">17</a> give $\left\|[Z_{i-1},v_l]\right\|_{2,\tau}\leq2\sqrt{E_{i-1}}$. Also $Z_{i-1}v_lr=[Z_{i-1},v_l]r$, proving <a href="#r:eq:old-overlap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="r:eq:old-overlap">[r:eq:old-overlap]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
###### One round of sampling and insertion.

<a id="r:eq:sample-mass-errors"></a>
Within this round we suppress $i$ from the block and signed-index notation, and process the finite signed index set $a=(j,\pm)$ in any fixed order. Both indices for $j$ have the target proportion $$c_a=\eta w_j/2.$$ Let $z_a$ be the occupied projection just before index $a$, starting with $Z_{i-1}$. We choose $$y_a=U_j(1_{k_j}\otimes e_a)U_j^*,\qquad e_a\leq q_j,$$ so that, uniformly over all $\tau\in\operatorname T(D)$, <a id="r:eq:sample-overlap-errors"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \sum_a|\tau(y_a)-c_a\tau(p_j)|&\leq\eta^2,
       \label{r:eq:sample-mass-errors}\\
 \sum_a|\tau(z_ay_a)-c_a\tau(z_ap_j)|&\leq\eta^2.
       \label{r:eq:sample-overlap-errors}
\end{aligned}$$ Here $j$ always denotes the block index of $a$. To make these choices precise, assign positive budgets $\varepsilon_a$ with $\sum_a\varepsilon_a\leq\eta^2$ before processing the family. At step $a$, apply Corollary <a href="#r:proj:tests" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="r:proj:tests">11</a> with proportion $c_a$, tests $q_j$ and $$h_a=\sum_{d=1}^{k_j}(U_j^*z_aU_j)_{dd}\in q_jDq_j,$$ and tolerance $\varepsilon_a/k_j$. Traciality gives $$\tau(y_a)=k_j\tau(e_a),\qquad
 \tau(z_ay_a)=\tau(e_ah_a),\qquad
 \tau(z_ap_j)=\tau(q_jh_a).$$ Thus both errors at this step are at most $\varepsilon_a$. The test $h_a$, which satisfies $0\leq h_a\leq k_jq_j$, is known when it is used. This application may refine $q_j$ internally, but only returns a subprojection $e_a$; it does not change the fixed weighted family.

<a id="r:eq:movement"></a>
Use Lemma <a href="#r:proj:move" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="r:proj:move">12</a> to replace $y_a$ by a projection $t_a\leq1-z_a$, retaining its sign, such that $$\label{r:eq:movement}
 \tau(y_a)-3\tau(z_ay_a)\leq\tau(t_a)\leq\tau(y_a),
 \qquad
 \left\|t_a-y_a\right\|_{2,\tau}^2\leq5\tau(z_ay_a).$$ Update the occupied projection to $z_a+t_a$. At the end of the round this is $Z_i$.

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
###### Mass and overlap.

<a id="r:eq:round-mass"></a>
<a id="r:eq:overlap-summable"></a>
For the estimates in this round, fix $\tau\in\operatorname T(D)$, and put $$C_i(\tau)=\sum_a c_a\tau(p_j),\quad
 Y_i(\tau)=\sum_a\tau(y_a),\quad
 L_i(\tau)=\sum_a\tau(z_ay_a).$$ These are the target mass, sampled mass, and overlap with the occupied projection in this round. All subsequent bounds hold for every $\tau$ with the same constants; we suppress $\tau$ in these three quantities. Equations <a href="#r:eq:diagonal-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="r:eq:diagonal-mass">[r:eq:diagonal-mass]</a> and <a href="#r:eq:block-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="r:eq:block-mass">[r:eq:block-mass]</a> give $$\eta\tau(r)-2\eta^3\leq C_i\leq\eta(1+\eta^2)\leq2\eta.$$ Using <a href="#r:eq:sample-mass-errors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="r:eq:sample-mass-errors">[r:eq:sample-mass-errors]</a>, we obtain $$\label{r:eq:round-mass}
 \eta\tau(r)-3\eta^2\leq Y_i\leq3\eta.$$ For the overlap, first use $z_a\leq Z_i$, then apply the single weighted-operator estimate <a href="#r:eq:block-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="r:eq:block-mass">[r:eq:block-mass]</a>: <a id="r:eq:round-overlap"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 L_i
 &\leq\eta^2+\eta\sum_jw_j\tau(Z_ip_j)\notag\\
 &\leq\eta^2+\eta\big(\eta^2+\tau(Z_i\Phi_i(r))\big)\notag\\
 &\leq\eta^2+\eta\big(\eta^2+4E_{i-1}+Y_i\big)\notag\\
 &\leq4\eta E_{i-1}+5\eta^2.
 \label{r:eq:round-overlap}
\end{aligned}$$ In the third line we used <a href="#r:eq:old-overlap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="r:eq:old-overlap">[r:eq:old-overlap]</a>, $0\leq\Phi_i(r)\leq1$, and $\tau(Z_i-Z_{i-1})\leq Y_i$, the last following from <a href="#r:eq:movement" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="r:eq:movement">[r:eq:movement]</a>. In particular, $$\label{r:eq:overlap-summable}
 L_i\leq E_i-E_{i-1}.$$

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
###### Commutators and linear tests.

<a id="r:eq:round-linear"></a>
<a id="r:eq:remaining-mass"></a>
For $x\in K_i$, sum Lemma <a href="#r:packing:sampling" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="r:packing:sampling">16</a> over this round. Equations <a href="#r:eq:sample-mass-errors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="r:eq:sample-mass-errors">[r:eq:sample-mass-errors]</a> and <a href="#r:eq:round-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="r:eq:round-mass">[r:eq:round-mass]</a> give <a id="r:eq:round-sample-energy"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \sum_a|\tau(y_ax)-c_a\tau(p_jx)|
 &\leq\eta(Y_i+C_i)+2\eta^2
 \leq7\eta^2,\label{r:eq:round-linear}\\
 \sum_a\left\|[x,y_a]\right\|_{2,\tau}^2
 &\leq\eta\sum_jw_j\left\|[x,p_j]\right\|_{2,\tau}^2
       +6\eta(Y_i+C_i)+10\eta^2\notag\\
 &\leq46\eta^2\leq60\eta^2.
 \label{r:eq:round-sample-energy}
\end{aligned}$$ Here the first term in the second estimate is at most $6\eta^2$ by <a href="#r:eq:block-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="r:eq:block-energy">[r:eq:block-energy]</a>. Passing to the inserted projections, <a href="#r:eq:movement" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="r:eq:movement">[r:eq:movement]</a> implies <a id="r:eq:round-inserted-energy"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \sum_a\left\|[x,t_a]\right\|_{2,\tau}^2
 &\leq2\sum_a\left\|[x,y_a]\right\|_{2,\tau}^2
        +8\sum_a\left\|t_a-y_a\right\|_{2,\tau}^2\notag\\
 &\leq120\eta^2+40L_i\notag\\
 &\leq160\eta E_{i-1}+320\eta^2
 \leq E_i-E_{i-1}.
 \label{r:eq:round-inserted-energy}
\end{aligned}$$ Together with $K_i\subseteq K_{i-1}$, this proves <a href="#r:eq:energy-induction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="r:eq:energy-induction">[r:eq:energy-induction]</a>. The mass left after the round satisfies $$\label{r:eq:remaining-mass}
 \tau(1-Z_i)\leq(1-\eta)\tau(1-Z_{i-1})+3\eta^2+3L_i,$$ by <a href="#r:eq:round-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="r:eq:round-mass">[r:eq:round-mass]</a> and the first inequality in <a href="#r:eq:movement" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="r:eq:movement">[r:eq:movement]</a>. All sums in this argument are controlled by weighted projection masses or energies. No bound on the raw sum $\sum_jw_j$, or on the number of blocks, has been used.

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
###### Completing the packing.

<a id="r:eq:final-energy"></a>
<a id="r:eq:final-remainder"></a>
<a id="r:eq:final-balance"></a>
Let $P,S$ be the sums of all inserted projections labelled $+$ and $-$, respectively. Then $P+S=Z_N$, and for $x\in K$, $\tau\in\operatorname T(D)$, $$\begin{aligned}
 \left\|[x,P]\right\|_{2,\tau}^2&\leq E_N,\label{r:eq:final-energy}\\
 \tau(1-Z_N)&\leq e^{-H}+3N\eta^2+3E_N,
       \label{r:eq:final-remainder}\\
 |\tau(Px)-\tau(Sx)|
 &\leq7N\eta^2+2\sqrt{15HE_N}.
       \label{r:eq:final-balance}
\end{aligned}$$ The first follows from Lemma <a href="#r:packing:orthogonal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="r:packing:orthogonal">17</a>. For the second, iterate <a href="#r:eq:remaining-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="r:eq:remaining-mass">[r:eq:remaining-mass]</a>, use $(1-\eta)^N\leq e^{-H}$, and sum <a href="#r:eq:overlap-summable" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="r:eq:overlap-summable">[r:eq:overlap-summable]</a>. For the third, the $+$ and $-$ targets $c_a\tau(p_jx)$ cancel for every block in every round. Their total sampling error is at most $7N\eta^2$, by <a href="#r:eq:round-linear" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="r:eq:round-linear">[r:eq:round-linear]</a>. The remaining displacement error is, by Lemma <a href="#r:proj:move" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="r:proj:move">12</a> and Cauchy–Schwarz over all signed indices, $$\begin{aligned}
 \sum_{i,a}|\tau((t_a-y_a)x)|
 &\leq2\sum_{i,a}\sqrt{\tau(y_a)}
                          \left\|t_a-y_a\right\|_{2,\tau}\\
 &\leq2\sqrt{\sum_iY_i}\sqrt{5\sum_iL_i}
 \leq2\sqrt{15HE_N}.
\end{aligned}$$

<a id="r:eq:halving-error"></a>
Solving <a href="#r:eq:energy-recurrence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="r:eq:energy-recurrence">[r:eq:energy-recurrence]</a> gives $$E_N=\eta\big((1+1000\eta)^N-1\big)\leq\eta e^{1000H},
 \qquad N\eta^2=H\eta.$$ Since $x$ is a contraction, $|\tau((1-Z_N)x)|\leq\tau(1-Z_N)$. Equations <a href="#r:eq:final-remainder" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="r:eq:final-remainder">[r:eq:final-remainder]</a> and <a href="#r:eq:final-balance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="r:eq:final-balance">[r:eq:final-balance]</a> therefore yield $$\label{r:eq:halving-error}
 \left|\tau(Px)-\tfrac12\tau(x)\right|
 \leq\tfrac12e^{-H}+5H\eta+\tfrac32E_N+\sqrt{15HE_N}.$$ Choose $H$ large enough to make the first term small. With this $H$ fixed, let $N$ increase through integers and put $\eta=H/N$. Then every other term in <a href="#r:eq:halving-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="r:eq:halving-error">[r:eq:halving-error]</a>, and $\sqrt{E_N}$ in <a href="#r:eq:final-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="r:eq:final-energy">[r:eq:final-energy]</a>, tends to zero. This proves the two desired uniform estimates. ◻

</details>

<a id="r:sec:completion"></a>
### The projection in the prescribed ultrapower

We now pass from the finite-set result to one projection, keeping the designated trace family and the fixed ultrafilter throughout.

<a id="stmt-r-completion-detection"></a>
<div id="r:completion:detection" class="lemma">

<a id="r:eq:limit-two"></a>
<a id="r:eq:limit-linear"></a>
**Lemma 18** (Detection by limit traces). *For a bounded sequence $d=(d_j)$ in $B$, with class $[d]\in B^\omega$, one has $$\begin{aligned}
 \sup_{\lambda\in\Lambda_\omega}\lambda([d]^*[d])
 &=\lim_{j\to\omega}\sup_{\tau\in\operatorname T(A)}
             \bar\tau(d_j^*d_j),\label{r:eq:limit-two}\\
 \sup_{\lambda\in\Lambda_\omega}|\lambda([d])|
 &=\lim_{j\to\omega}\sup_{\tau\in\operatorname T(A)}
             |\bar\tau(d_j)|.\label{r:eq:limit-linear}
\end{aligned}$$ In particular, if $\left\|z\right\|_{2,\lambda}=0$ for every $\lambda\in\Lambda_\omega$, then $z=0$ in $B^\omega$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-r-completion-detection"><summary>Proof of stmt-r-completion-detection</summary>

*Proof.* The upper bounds follow from the definition of a limit trace. For the reverse bound in <a href="#r:eq:limit-two" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="r:eq:limit-two">[r:eq:limit-two]</a>, choose $\tau_j$ whose value on $d_j^*d_j$ is within $1/j$ of the supremum. The associated limit trace gives equality. For <a href="#r:eq:limit-linear" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="r:eq:limit-linear">[r:eq:limit-linear]</a>, choose approximate maximizers of $|\bar\tau(d_j)|$. Absolute value commutes with the ultralimit of a bounded complex sequence, so this choice also gives equality. The final assertion is <a href="#r:eq:limit-two" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="r:eq:limit-two">[r:eq:limit-two]</a> and the definition of $I_\omega$. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-r-completion-detection-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-r-completion-detection</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#r:thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="r:thm:main">1</a>.* Set $D=B^\omega$. It is a unital real-rank-zero $C^*$-algebra by hypothesis and has the tracial states in $\Lambda_\omega$. The inclusion $A\subseteq D$ is unital and faithful. Lemma <a href="#r:proj:small" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="r:proj:small">8</a> supplies full projections of uniformly arbitrarily small trace over all $\operatorname T(D)$. Thus Theorem <a href="#r:thm:finite" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="r:thm:finite">3</a> applies.

<a id="r:eq:finite-stage"></a>
Choose increasing finite subsets $F_n\subseteq\mathcal U(A)$ whose union is norm dense in $\mathcal U(A)$, including $1$ in each $F_n$. Theorem <a href="#r:thm:finite" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="r:thm:finite">3</a> gives projections $P_n\in D$ with $$\label{r:eq:finite-stage}
 \left\|[P_n,x]\right\|_{2,\tau}<1/n,\qquad
 \left|\tau(P_nx)-\tfrac12\tau(x)\right|<1/n
 \quad(x\in F_n,\ \tau\in\operatorname T(D)).$$ Lift $P_n$ to positive contractions $(c_{n,j})_j$ in $B$.
